She bowed, saying this. The Ammai, a mother who understood the depth of maternal love, had only that one thought in her mind: how to obtain such a child, the only son. Therefore, she asked that question. Her mind did not go beyond that to the level of doing something that is very difficult for anyone to do. But Siruthonda Nayanar was a man of strong will. Therefore, the Nayanar's mind went beyond that, and he thought that he had received a great blessing that was difficult to obtain from anyone else, in any way. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman shows us the action he decided to take in the next verse. The devotion of serving food to devotees was equally present in Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammai and Siruthonda Nayanar. However, in worldly life, the devotion of women and the devotion of men differ slightly. The devotion of men is firm and resolute. We see this explained here. Siruthonda Nayanar, looking at the face of his wife, Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammai, who had asked that question, said, "There are people in this world who have such a son. If you give them enough wealth, they will be ready to give him. But no father or mother in this world would knowingly give their own son. Therefore, without further delay, let us call our son, whom you bore to save me here, for this sacred act." Therefore, Siruthonda Nayanar, including his wife, says, "Let us call our son to be the 'pasu' for the Bhairava's sacred food in this matter." The phrase "Thammil Manam Uvanthe" (with joyful hearts) indicates that the love of the two, Siruthonda Nayanar and Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammai, was already united in their intention to perform this act. Therefore, Siruthonda Nayanar uses the plural "we will call." Hearing this, Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammai, with consent, and without further delay, says to Siruthonda Nayanar, "We must prepare the sacred food and see the joyful face of that Bhairava devotee. Therefore, let us do this. Go to the school and bring our son, who is like a jewel that protects us." We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 09 — Siruthonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 932, authored by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam, Siruthonda Nayanar Puranam. The 57th verse: "When her husband said this, his wife, like a divine being, agreed and said, 'Let us see the joyful face of the Lord's devotee, who will partake of food without delay today. Go to the school and bring our son, who is like a jewel that protects us.'" This is the verse. When her husband, Siruthonda Nayanar, said, "Let us call our son for this service to the devotee," Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammai, agreeing to it, said, "Our duty now is to see the joyful face of our Lord Shiva's devotee, who will partake of food without delay today." Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammai, who was like a divine being, said to her husband, Siruthonda Nayanar, "Go to the school and bring our son, who is like a jewel that protects us." The phrase "malarntha mugam nanru kanpathu" (to see the joyful face) appears. The purpose of a feast is to see the happy face of the guest after he has eaten. Therefore, the Ammai considered it her great blessing to see the joyful face of the Bhairava devotee after he had partaken of the food. She also says that seeing it is their duty. For that sacred service, their son had gone to school. Sirala Devanar was to become an instrument for attaining liberation, helping to ensure that this vow of feeding the devotee was not broken, and thus assisting in this meritorious act. "Bring our son," says the Ammai, referring to such a son. He had gone to school. Siruthonda Nayanar, after meeting the devotee in the temple, did not go directly to the school to bring his son, Sirala Devar. Instead, he came to his house, asked his wife, obtained her consent, and now goes to the school to bring Sirala Devar. The Ammai who said this was like Thiruvai (Lakshmi). Thiruvai here refers to Mukti Thiru (the Lakshmi of liberation). The prime deity of that Mukti Thiru is Gnanaambigai. Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammai was like that Gnanaambigai. She said this. Now, the 58th verse: "When his loving wife said this, the husband, Siruthonda Nayanar, as if he had attained all the blessings, free from all faults, quickly went to the school where his sweet-voiced son, who spoke in soft, lisping words, had gone to study, to bring him for the Lord's sacred food." As soon as his loving wife said this, the husband, Siruthonda Nayanar, as if he had attained all the blessings, free from all faults, rejoiced and quickly went to the school where his sweet-voiced son, who spoke in soft, lisping words, had gone to study, to bring him for the Lord's sacred food. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman describes Sirala Devanar as a child whose mouth still smelled of milk, with a soft voice and sweet, lisping words, indicating Siruthonda Nayanar's immense love for Sirala Devar and emphasizing the significance of this act. The traditional belief is that the school where Sirala Devanar studied is in a village a short distance north of Thiruchengattangudi. As a memorial to that school where Sirala Devanar studied, there is a small temple for Sirala Devar there. In it, a beautiful child-like idol of Sirala Devar is installed and worshipped. Now, the 59th and 60th verses are as follows: "Going to the school, as his anklets jingled, the child ran and embraced him. He lifted him onto his shoulder and brought him back home. The virtuous wife went before her generous husband, received her son, arranged his hair, wiped his face, removed the dust from his earrings and waistband, lamented that the saffron paste had faded, moved the collyrium away from his eyes, and with great love, the Ammai, whose soft feet were like cotton, bathed him, adorned him perfectly, and gave him to her husband's hand." Siruthonda Nayanar goes to the school. As soon as Sirala Devanar saw his father, his feet, adorned with jingling anklets, made a sound, and the child ran and embraced him. This is what children do when they see their parents coming. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman, by singing that the anklets jingled as Sirala Devanar ran, implicitly worships those sacred feet. We see here that this running, which ends with an embrace, continues later when Sirala Devanar comes back to life by divine grace, as if he ran from school. Siruthonda Nayanar lifted the child who ran to him onto his shoulder and brought him home, so as not to delay the preparation of sacred food for the devotee. Moreover, this child was the offering for the devotee's sacred food. Therefore, if the child walked and his body suffered, his body would become weak and change. So, Siruthonda Nayanar carried him on his shoulder and returned home. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman praises Siruthonda Nayanar as a generous one in the verse, saying that he performed extraordinary acts and gave without limit. As Siruthonda Nayanar entered his sacred house with Sirala Devanar on his shoulder, Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammai, the virtuous wife, went before Siruthondar, the generous one, and received her son. The Ammai, out of great eagerness not to delay, quickly received the child. She took the child, arranged his hair, wiped his face, removed the dust from his earrings and waistband, lamented that the saffron paste he had worn earlier had faded, moved the collyrium away from his eyes so it would not spread, and with great love, the Ammai, whose soft feet were like cotton, bathed him, adorned him perfectly, and gave him to her husband's hand. Arranging the hair, wiping the face, and so on, are all acts that mothers lovingly perform as soon as they see their children. Therefore, it is said here that the Ammai did so according to such worldly custom. However, that body was to become the offering for the devotee's food. Therefore, these were the actions the Ammai performed with the intention of cleaning and beautifying that offering. She arranged his hair, wiped away the sweat and dirt from his earrings and waistband, and removed all the dust. The saffron paste mixed with kumkum, which he had worn when going to school, had faded due to play and sweat. She also corrected that, performing the adornment in such a way that there was no fault whatsoever. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman describes this faultless adornment in such detail because this beautiful, unique son will come back to life with the same adornment. Sirala Devanar will be seated in Kailash with Lord Shiva and Ambigai, never to be separated, as their son Murugapperuman, between them. Therefore, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman sings this divine adornment in great detail. The Ammai herself received the child whom her husband, Siruthonda Nayanar, had brought from school. She adorned him. She gave him back to Siruthonda Nayanar's hand. The wife's duty was to wash the child's body and make it suitable for sacred food. This was done out of her love and the need to make it suitable for sacred food. The Ammai's act of adorning Sirala Devanar and giving him to her husband's hand shows her state of being rooted in a great love for Shiva's servitude, transcending worldly norms. After this, when he was called and came back to life as her son, the Ammai will again embrace Sirala Devanar, the Nayanar, and give him to her husband's hand. We are about to see that. That is the worldly love she had as his mother. Here, she gave him to her husband's hand for the next action, which men perform. There, she gave him to her husband's hand because the Bhairava had said, "We will eat if he comes." The Bhairava devotee is about to say, "We will eat only if your son comes." Therefore, to suit that, her son had come. So, she will show that son to the devotee and make the devotee eat. For that reason, the Ammai rejoices and embraces her son who comes back to life. That too is out of her great love for the devotees. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 10 — Siruthonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Volume 933, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. The chapter "Varkonda Vanamulaiyal" contains the Puranam of Siruthonda Nayanar. Four continuous verses, numbered 61, 62, 63, and 64. Fearing that the meat would become an offering, they did not kiss their precious son, nor embrace him to their chest, nor kiss him. The blameless Siruthondar and his wife, with eager hearts, went to prepare the meat for the holy ones, taking their son to a secluded spot where the world would not know. The mother, who had given birth to the child, cleaned the vessels well. The father, who had conquered the world, held his good son's head. The true mother quickly pulled his sweet, jingling feet onto her lap and held the child's fruit-like mouth and hands. The loving son, seeing their great joy, smiled. The father, with a tool, severed the son's head. The unparalleled greatness of the son's true nature shone forth, and he attained joy. His wife, too, rejoiced greatly, thinking, "My husband has given me his precious life." Thus, both their hearts were filled with immense joy, and they performed this rare act, as described in the verses. There is a rule that one should not partake in any way of the items offered to Lord Shiva and His devotees. One must protect them from being partaken. Partaking means smelling with the nose, looking with desire, touching, or any other way of experiencing it. One must offer the sacred food with fear that it might be partaken in some way. For this reason, they did not kiss their son, Siraaladevanayanar, nor embrace him, nor kiss him, because he was the item that would be used for the sacred food for the Siruthondar. Siraaladevanayanar's mother and father did not do these things. Kissing the forehead, embracing, and kissing are all acts by which parents express joy and affection for their children. The verse mentions "mutham unnal" (eating a kiss), which refers to both giving and receiving a kiss with joy. In "Pillai Tamil," we see "mutham tharugave" (please give a kiss). Here, they changed their perception of their son from being their own to being a sacred offering for the devotees. Therefore, the state of joy associated with these acts changed. They feared that if they kissed him, embraced him, or partook of his kiss, the meat offering would become defiled. So, without doing any of these things, the blameless Siruthondar and his wife, with great desire to prepare the meat for the holy devotees, took their son not to the kitchen but to another secluded place. The devotee who had come was a holy person, a purifier, pure. Therefore, the place where the food was prepared for him also had to be holy. So, they took their son to a separate, private place, thinking that if they performed the necessary acts in the kitchen, it would diminish the sanctity. The two, with united minds, went to a secluded place, thinking that the people of the world would not understand the truth of this. They took the child with them. If the people of the world were to see what Siruthonda Nayanar and his wife were doing, they might, out of ignorance, condemn it, obstruct it, or cause hindrance. This would cause delay and hinder the preparation of the sacred food for the devotee. Therefore, they went to a secluded place to perform the act. Thus, their eagerness was to quickly prepare their son's body for the sacred food. The mother, Thiruvengattu Nangaiyar, cleaned all the vessels well.

Good vessels suitable for meat and other items. It is best if they are made of precious metals like gold and silver. They must be purified. The vessels and containers used for the sacred food must be well-cleaned and purified. Otherwise, medical and culinary texts state that the sacred food will be spoiled and ruined by impurities, rust, and other defects. Therefore, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman's inclusion of these worldly teachings in this narrative demonstrates his exalted poetic excellence. The nature of Siraaladevanayanar is to be instrumental in preparing sacred food for the Lord and to help parents uphold their vow of feeding devotees without fail, thereby attaining a higher state. Furthermore, the greatness of Thirugnanasambandar Peruman, the child of the Lord, is his magnanimity. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman uses the phrase "good son" with all these meanings embedded within it. We saw him as the divine child Siraaladevar. Indeed, who is a good son? Even in worldly terms, a son who brings great joy to his parents is called a good son. Therefore, Siraaladevanar is a good son. His father is a father who conquered the world. In worldly life, the love for one's child, known as "puthira vanchai," is an extremely difficult passion to overcome. We can recall the story of Dasaratha here. But here, the father is one who has conquered the world. Such a father, Siruthonda Nayanar, holds the head of Siraaladevanayanar. The true mother quickly pulls his sweet, jingling feet onto her lap and holds the child's fruit-like mouth and hands. The son, that good son, smiles, thinking how greatly his parents are rejoicing. The parents' hearts were so joyful that the son, seeing the happiness on his parents' faces, felt immense joy and laughed himself. This is the point here. Thus, Siruthonda Nayanar performs that act which is very rare in this world. Siruthonda Nayanar and his wife perform an act that everyone would fear to do, an act that no one would dare to do in this worldly context. This means they are doing it knowingly. They are doing it knowing that these are the exalted acts, the conclusions of spiritual texts. But the worldly people will not understand these things. Even though Siruthonda Nayanar and his wife performed this act, which might appear to be an act of murder, it is not murder. It is a spiritual act performed through the grace of the Lord. How so? If the Nayanar and his wife had not consented to this act, their son would have lived on this earth for a few more days like other humans, then died, and then been reborn and died again, stuck in that cycle. But because of this rare act, this rare deed, he was freed from the bonds of birth and attained a state of eternal union with the Lord, never to be separated. Therefore, just as any action is judged by its outcome, this act brought Siruthonda Nayanar, Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammaiyar, Siraaladevanayanar, and Santhanathathaiyar to the feet of the Lord. Therefore, this is a spiritual accomplishment, not a murderous act as worldly people might call it. This is the conclusion and divine will of the wise. It is the truth stated in spiritual scriptures. Through this rare act, these two, the parents of Siraaladevanayanar, were freed from worldly bonds and attained that state. We must carefully consider how the histories of all the Nayanmars, starting with Iyarpagai Nayanar, are structured in the Periyapuranam.

The belief that inflicting pain on the human body and separating life from it is always, in any circumstance, an act of violence against living beings and murder, is the doctrine of pseudo-Jains and Buddhists. Even leaders of communities, those called intellectuals, are deluded by this false doctrine. We have seen the truth of this in many places in the Periyapuranam. The commentator further explains this. When there is a disease in the body, a doctor uses instruments to cut, dissect, separate, and amputate the affected organs, performing medical procedures. We give great wealth to such a doctor, delighted by his actions and the treatment he provides. Even during childbirth, when a natural delivery is not possible, we see doctors dismembering and severing the child to save the mother. All these actions of the doctor help to preserve the body for a few more days, a few more years, and to live well in this world for a longer period. But these rare acts performed by our Nayanmars are actions that lead to eternal dwelling at the feet of the Lord. If that is the case, then they are in no way wrong. Therefore, only those who do not understand this truth will preach doctrines of false compassion for living beings. Henceforth, the services rendered to God and His devotees become supreme Shivapunyam (meritorious acts). That divine merit will endure until the soul attains liberation and will further enable the performance of more Shivapunyam. Therefore, this does not fall under the category of sin (paathakam) but under the category of acts of grace (arutcheyal). In the Puranam of Thirukannappadeva Nayanar, we saw that he hunted and killed animals to prepare sacred food for Shiva Puja. We saw there how the divine Sekkizhar Peruman explained it as an act of grace, "killed and graced." Therefore, Kannappa Nayanar killing other animals for Shiva Puja was indeed an act of grace, as we saw in detail there. In the Puranam of Thandiyadigal Nayanar, when he was digging a pond for the Lord, Jains came and said that the creatures in the soil would suffer, so he should not renovate the pond. At that time, it was said that doing so would also be a Shivapunyam. It would benefit those creatures and accumulate Shivapunyam. Now, the undeniable truth is that the good or bad that comes from an object is determined by the good or bad of the actions performed with that object. The verse states, "If the calf whose life was lost under the moving chariot, its great sin will be removed today, so he sought his son and the case of the one who rode over him, like poison in the mind, the messengers of Yama with fierce anger, seeing the suffering in the other hell, praise the tree of compassion." Thus, such an act is praised as a tree of compassion. This is what the spiritual scriptures say. Here, Siruthonda Nayanar and his wife believed that these worldly objects, bodies, and lives are all the Lord's possessions. To think of them as our own is delusion. They understood the truth that these belong to Arana and Arana's devotees, and that making them available to the Lord and His devotees is the conclusion of the spiritual scriptures, and they lived accordingly. Those who lived with the clear light of great wisdom, understanding that such a life ensures the well-being of the soul, are our great true Nayanmars. All such true Nayanmars were Jivanmuktas (liberated souls) who lived in that manner. Therefore, all their actions are acts of grace. It is a great mistake to measure their actions with the worldly yardstick. Therefore, Siruthonda Nayanar and his wife rejoiced greatly, understanding that "the unparalleled, great son has given me his true nature." When the Nayanar attained that joy, his wife also rejoiced with a blossoming heart, thinking, "This son has given me my husband's precious life." Thus, both their hearts were filled with immense joy.

They performed that rare act. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 11 — Siruthonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Volume 934, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. The chapter "Varkonda Vanamulaiyal" contains the Puranam of Siruthonda Nayanar. The 65th verse. The meat from the head was deemed unsuitable for the sacred offering and was set aside. Santhanathathaiyar was given it to dispose of. All the other meat from the body was chopped, cut, and the bones and brain were removed. Various spices needed for the curry were ground and added, and the curry was quickly prepared, as stated in the 65th verse. The lady with fragrant, flowing hair, joyfully cooked the curry on the stove. Knowing its perfect state, she removed it and tempered it in another precious vessel with fragrant spices. She quickly prepared various other curries and also cooked the rice. Then she informed her husband. These are the two verses, 65 and 66. The mother gives the meat from the head to Santhanathathaiyar, asking her to set it aside in another place, as it is not suitable for the sacred offering. All the other meat from the body is properly prepared for the sacred offering, with various spices like asafoetida, fennel, cinnamon, and other aromatic ingredients added. The mother eagerly prepares the sacred food, desiring to quickly appease the hunger of the Lord's devotee. She places them in a vessel on the stove and, with a joyful heart, cooks the curry. Knowing its perfect state, she removes it from the stove and tempers it in another good vessel with appropriate ingredients. She ensures that the tempering is fragrant, not burnt, melted, or charred, but perfectly seasoned and aromatic. She quickly cooks the rice as well. Then she informs her husband, the Nayanar. At this point, the wife's tasks are complete. Now, the devotee must be invited to partake of the sacred food. Therefore, Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammaiyar informs her husband. The 67th verse. "The sacred food has been prepared as the Lord commanded for His meal." Siruthonda Nayanar, with even greater joy and enthusiasm than before, quickly went to the holy one who was seated under the Arathi tree, where bees hummed around the soft flowers. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to the devotee as "Udaiya Nadhar" (the Lord) in this verse, following the tradition of considering a devotee as the Lord Himself. We see in this story how that devotion was fulfilled. Therefore, it is explained here that when a devotee is fed, Lord Paramashiva Himself partakes of the food from within them. The joy of having the devotee state what sacred food he desired, and then having that desire fulfilled, and the sacred food prepared, filled him with overflowing joy. Bees were buzzing around the Arathi flowers, waiting for them to bloom to drink their nectar. Siruthonda Nayanar went before the holy one, the Bhairava devotee, who was seated under such an Arathi tree, and bowed to him. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to the Bhairava devotee as "holy one." Generally, eating meat, especially human flesh, and being the cause of a five-year-old boy's murder, and furthermore, the parents themselves holding the child to facilitate this act, are all considered unholy acts in the world. Even though the devotee performed all these acts, his holiness was not diminished. Sekkizhar Peruman uses this name to indicate that the holy one is indeed holy. Verses 68 and 69. Approaching the Lord, he bowed and the devotee said, "I humbly request that you partake of the sacred food here with me. I desired this with love. Although it caused a delay for you to be hungry, I have prepared the offering as commanded. Please come and fulfill my wish." He then said, "Please come quickly and partake of the sacred food." The Lord, who had concealed the darkness of the poison in His throat and hidden His third eye on His forehead, then said, "Siruthondar, whose greatness is complete, enough!" and agreed. He was like a poor man who had received two treasures, Sanghanidhi and Padumanidhi, and with such joy, he entered his house. The devotee Siruthondar approaches the Lord and bows, then speaks. "I lovingly requested that you come here and partake of the sacred food with me. For this, you had to wait, being hungry, for a long time. As per your command, I have prepared the sacred food for you. Please come and fulfill my wish." He requests, "My desire is to somehow feed you the sacred food. To fulfill this, please come and partake of the sacred food, just as you have decided in your divine will to take us as your own, so that we may receive that grace." He then bows, saying, "Now, without any further delay, please come and partake of the sacred food." The Lord, who had come in the guise of a Bhairava devotee, concealed the blackness of the poison in His throat and hid His third eye on His forehead. He then said, "Siruthondar, whose greatness is complete, enough!" and consented. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman says that the Lord concealed the darkness in His throat and the third eye on His forehead. Earlier, he had described in detail the appearance of the Bhairava devotee. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman reiterates it to bring that complete divine form back to our minds by pointing out these two aspects. We saw that He wore an ornament with ambrosial bubbles to conceal the poison in His throat. The Thiruneelakandam (blue throat) of the Lord is a symbol of His grace. Similarly, He concealed the third eye on His forehead. He hid it so it was not visible. We saw that He adorned His forehead with sacred ash like a dot, concealing the third eye, thus appearing in the guise of a Bhairava devotee. Concealing the Thiruneelakandam is general. Concealing the third eye on the forehead is special. The grace of the third eye is special to the outcome of this story, as we saw in the very first verse of this Puranam. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman sings, "He concealed the darkness in His throat and did not show the eye on His forehead." We also recall here the verse from Irupa Irupattu, "He concealed the eye on His forehead and the darkness in His throat, and removed the impurities of the people on earth." The Lord who came thus bestows grace upon Siruthonda Nayanar. He says, "Siruthondar, the time for your greatness to be complete has come," and then rises to depart. At that moment, Siruthonda Nayanar rejoiced, as a very poor man would rejoice if he were given the two treasures, Sanghanidhi and Padumanidhi. Verses 70 and 71. The Bhairava devotee entered the beautiful house. The wife went forward to greet him, bowed at his feet, and welcomed him. The house was already beautifully decorated. Fragrant flower garlands and pearl garlands were hung. A seat adorned with fragrant flowers was also prepared. Siruthonda Nayanar had gone to invite the Bhairava devotee and was now bringing him. During this interim, the wife had arranged all these decorations. It was already a well-decorated house, but she had adorned it even more beautifully. This was not for herself. It was their custom to decorate their house in this manner daily for the devotees they fed. Now, the Bhairava Jangamar was about to arrive. Therefore, for his sake, she had adorned the house with fragrant flower garlands and pearl garlands. She bowed to the Jangama devotee who had arrived and showed him the flower-adorned seat, requesting him to sit there. Showing the seat. The seat, the deity, and the worshipper are parts of the puja. Providing a seat is the first part of the three parts of the puja. We can recall the Shiva Agama puja rituals here. Here, the deity is the sacred form of the Bhairava devotee. The worshipper is Lord Shiva, who resides within the devotee's form and accepts the puja. Thus, the Bhairava devotee sits on that seat. Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammaiyar takes a pot filled with water and holds it. A pot is a small vessel with a spout. It is also called a Kundigai. The small spout helps to pour water continuously. It is also customary to put flowers, saffron, and other aromatic ingredients in the water. Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammaiyar holds the pot and pours water. As the mother pours water, Siruthonda Nayanar washes the feet of the devotee. It is customary for the wife to pour water and the husband to wash the feet of the devotee. We will see this in the Puranam of Kalikkamba Nayanar as well. Washing the feet in this manner is called Paathyam, a part of the puja. The water used to wash the feet becomes sacred water (theertham). They sprinkle that pure theertham abundantly over their heads, filling their hearts with joy. They sprinkle the theertham all over the house and rejoice. That beautiful house is used for Shiva dharmas, for the worship of devotees and the Lord. Therefore, it is a house filled with joy, not a house filled with worldly pleasures of attachment and material possessions. It is a house where the supreme Shiva bliss, derived from Shiva dharmas like the worship of devotees and the Lord, resides. With the thought that the long-lasting good fortune of that house should continue to yield such good results, they sprinkle the theertham throughout the house. They offer worship with fragrant, soft flowers. They apply sandalwood paste and offer incense and lamps. Offering flowers, showing incense, and holding lamps are parts of the puja. Thus, Siruthonda Nayanar and his wife perform the puja, including all these rituals, and worship the Bhairava devotee. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 12 — Siruthonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Volume 935, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. The chapter "Varkonda Vanamulaiyal" contains the Puranam of Siruthonda Nayanar. Verses 72, 73, and 74. Siruthonda Nayanar and his wife bowed before the holy Bhairava, whose matted hair, adorned with the cool white moon, spread out like a beautiful, flower-decked coiffure, and asked how they should serve the sacred food and rice. The Bhairava replied, "Serve all the curries along with the sweet rice." So, to make the offering clear, they arranged the vessels, placed them on a cloth, and clearly presented the rich rice made from red paddy and the various curries in order. They then placed the serving vessel on a white cloth spread over a tripod. The Lord, seeing this, said, "Did you prepare the meat of the sacrificed animal according to the method I described, with all its parts, and make it delicious?" When the wife replied, "We removed the head-meat, thinking it was unsuitable for the sacred offering," the Lord, who removes the troubles of devotees, said, "That too, I usually eat; it is suitable for me." These are the verses. After bringing the Bhairava Jangama into the house and performing all the pujas, Siruthonda Nayanar and his wife bowed and requested the pure Lord Bhairava, whose matted hair was adorned with the cool white crescent moon and spread out like a beautiful, flower-decked coiffure. They asked, "Please tell us how we should serve the sacred food to you." The Bhairava had already indicated what kind of sacred food he desired. Therefore, they now requested him to tell them how to serve that food appropriately, so that no mistake would occur. To those who made this request, the Bhairava devotee replied, "Serve the rice and all the curries together, as one." So, to make the offering clear, they arranged the eating vessel, placed it on a cloth, and clearly presented the rich rice made from red paddy and the various curries in order. They then took the serving vessel and placed it on a white cloth spread over a tripod. They arranged the offered curries clearly, so their nature was evident. The tripod, a three-legged stand, is called "parikkalakkal." They spread a cloth over it and placed the sacred food in good vessels. After the sacred food was offered, the Lord, the Immaculate One, looked at it and asked Siruthonda Nayanar, "Did you prepare the meat of the sacrificed animal according to the method I described, with all its parts, and make it delicious?" He asked if all the parts were cooked and offered without fail. To this, the mother replied, "We removed the head-meat, thinking it was unsuitable for the sacred offering." Upon hearing this, the Lord, who removes the troubles of devotees, said, "That too, I usually eat; it is suitable for me." He said, "Even that which you and others discard is what I eat." Upon hearing this, Siruthonda Nayanar's mind was troubled. The 75th verse. Siruthondar, with his wife, was distressed and bewildered. Then Santhanathathaiyar, their maid, said, "I prepared the head-meat as a curry, thinking that the devotee who came to bestow grace might remember it when partaking of the sacred food." As she presented it, their faces blossomed with joy. Siruthonda Nayanar's mind was troubled, and he and his wife were saddened. Then Santhanathathaiyar, their maidservant, said, "I prepared the head-meat as a curry, thinking that the holy devotee who came to bestow grace might remember it when partaking of the sacred food, and he might even accept it as sacred food." As she said this and presented it, Siruthonda Nayanar and his wife's faces blossomed with joy. Why were they distressed? They were troubled and bewildered, thinking that despite doing all these rare things that the Bhairava had asked for, the sacred food was being delayed due to this shortcoming. Those who have friends, servants, and attendants who help them in times of need, like a staff for support or a hand for one who has lost his garment, will never face any difficulty. Therefore, the rare state of service is to act without being asked, understanding the unspoken wish. Thus, by the skill of serving the devotees by understanding their unspoken wishes, and by the meritorious deeds of Shiva performed by Siruthonda Nayanar and Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammaiyar, and by the merit of serving below them, Santhanathathaiyar also attains liberation. If Santhanathathaiyar also attains the liberation that Siruthonda Nayanar, Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammaiyar, and Siraaladevar attained, and if Santhanathathaiyar also receives the blessing of accompanying them to Thirukailayam, it is due to this rare and great act of Shiva Punya. Four verses, 76, 77, 78, and 79. Siruthondar, who had received the head-meat with joy and offered it, was then addressed by the Bhairava, who said, "I cannot eat alone here. Bring any devotees of Shiva who are nearby." Siruthondar, thinking, "Am I ruined? Is this another obstacle to his meal?" went out, but by divine grace, he could not find any devotees anywhere. His face filled with great sadness, he humbly bowed and said to the Lord, "I cannot find anyone, neither in this world nor the next." He bowed and pleaded, "Are there any who wear sacred ash like me? Please eat." The Lord then commanded Thiruvengattu Ammai, who was of perfect chastity, to prepare the vessel and serve the hot meat and rice to him as well. When Siruthondar tried to feed the devotee, the devotee stopped him and said, "We will eat after six months. Until then, we will not eat. You eat rice daily. Why are you eating before us? If you have a son who can eat with us, call him." Siruthondar replied, "He is of no use to those who have neither beginning nor end now." Siruthondar, having received the head-meat from Santhanathathaiyar with joy, offered it to the Bhairava devotee and stood bowing. The Bhairava devotee, addressing Siruthondar, who was requesting him to partake of the sacred food, said, "I cannot eat alone now. If there are any devotees of Lord Shiva nearby, please bring them." The Bhairava devotee commanded this. Generally, it is also a rule in worldly dharma texts not to eat alone. There is a rule in the Vedas and Shiva Agamas, the great texts of divine merit, that one should eat with devotees of Lord Shiva. Therefore, the Bhairava devotee tells Siruthonda Nayanar, "If there are any Shiva devotees nearby, bring them. I cannot eat alone." We have already seen that Siruthonda Nayanar had been searching for devotees throughout that day to perform Maheswara Puja at his home, but he could not find anyone. In this situation, how can I go and bring a devotee? Now, there is further delay. It is rare to find devotees now. Will he not eat then? With this anxiety rising in his mind, he laments, "Am I ruined?" and grieves. He goes outside the house to search, but by divine grace, he cannot find any devotee anywhere. With great sadness on his face, he comes before the Lord and humbly says, "I have searched for Shiva devotees, who are dear both in this world and the next, but I could not find them." It happens by divine grace. The fact that no devotees came that day and that he could not find any, and also that the son who had been made into curry would now return, both happened by divine grace. This section emphasizes the greatness of divine grace. Devotees are dear both in this world and the next. Guru, Lingam, Jangamam are the three forms of the Lord. Here, the benefit of worshipping Jangamas, the Shiva devotees, is clearly demonstrated. We see the special way in which this is conveyed through the words of the great Siruthondar. Therefore, Siruthonda Nayanar comes and requests, saying that he could not find any devotees. Wearing sacred ash is the characteristic of devotees. Therefore, he asks the Bhairava devotee, "I also wear sacred ash. Can I be accepted to eat with you?" Siruthondar's mind was not willing to consider himself as one of the Lord's devotees. Therefore, he asks, "Even though I am a humble person unworthy of being one of Shiva's devotees, since I wear sacred ash, can I be considered worthy of being counted among the devotees?" It is the custom of those who are exalted in Shaivism to consider sacred ash as the mark of a devotee. When he thus pleaded, the Bhairava devotee said, "Are there any who wear sacred ash like you?" The Lord Himself praises and highlights the excellence of Siruthondar's devotion to sacred ash. Therefore, the Bhairava devotee says, "You yourself eat with me." The Lord, who came as the Bhairava devotee, commanded Thiruvengattu Ammaiyar, who was of perfect chastity, to prepare the vessel and serve the rest of the meat and rice to him as well. The mother also served accordingly. Siruthonda Nayanar, desiring to quickly feed the Lord's devotee, the Bhairava devotee, and thinking that there had already been a delay and there should be no further delay, began to eat quickly. The Bhairava devotee stopped Siruthonda Nayanar, who was about to eat, and said, "We will eat after six months. Until then, we will not eat. You eat rice daily. Why are you eating before us?" He then added, "If you have an unparalleled son who can eat with us, call him." The Bhairava devotee, who said this, is the Lord who has neither beginning nor end. Siruthonda Nayanar replies to him, "He will not be of use now." Thus, he speaks in a way that does not contradict the truth. Hearing this, the Lord, the Bhairava devotee, says, "I will eat here only if your son comes. Therefore, call him." Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 13 — Siruthonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Volume 936, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. The chapter "Varkonda Vanamulaiyal" contains the Puranam of Siruthonda Nayanar. "We will eat here only after your son comes," says the Bhairava devotee. Verses 80, 81, and 82. "We will eat here if he comes, so call him," said the Lord. The leader, unable to bear it, thought, "What can I do if the Lord eats?" He quickly rose and, by grace, went out with his wife, who had soft, fragrant hair, to call him. As he was about to enter, Siruthondar called, "My son, come!" The lady, upholding her Lord's command, also called, "My precious jewel, Siraala! Come! The devotee of Shiva calls you to eat with him, so that we may be saved!" By the Lord's grace, he came running as if from school, a unique son of unparalleled beauty. The mother picked him up, embraced him, held him close to her, and gave him to her husband. They rejoiced, for they had received the blessing of feeding the devotee of the Lord who had burned the three cities. These are the verses. "We will eat here if he comes. Therefore, seek him out and call him," said the Lord, the Bhairava devotee. "We will eat only after he comes. Therefore, his coming is certain," he indicates here. Who indicated this? The Lord, who bestows grace upon those who trust in Him. Here, some researchers err in their research. They question, "Would Siruthonda Nayanar go out to call the son whom he had killed with his own hands?" This is because they research with their own consciousness. Here, the primary duty is to follow the path indicated by the Lord who came as the Bhairava devotee. To analyze whether it is possible or not, right or wrong, is not the tradition of the Shiva path. The Sivagnana Siddhiyar states, "With desire, approach the devotee of Arana, make his work your work, speak with humility, worship with the mark of divine wisdom, beat the drum, and dance." This describes the nature of those who have attained liberation. This is the nature of Jivanmuktas. Here, Siruthonda Nayanar and his wife are of this nature. Here, the devotee commanded, "Seek him out and call him." Siruthonda Nayanar and his wife received that command. They called him according to that command. The thought, "Will the son, whom Siruthonda Nayanar had said earlier would not be useful here, come?" did not even arise in the Nayanar's mind. "We will follow the devotee's command. We will follow his indication. Whatever happens next, let it happen by divine grace." This is the state of mind of the Nayanmars. This is the state of mind of Siruthonda Nayanar. Earlier, when the Bhairava devotee commanded, "Call the devotee who wears sacred ash to eat with us," even though no devotee could be found, he tried to fulfill that command, and a solution was found by divine grace. Therefore, now too, "We will follow the devotee's command. Whatever happens next will happen by divine grace." Thinking this, Siruthonda Nayanar and his wife do so. They call their son. Therefore, according to the Bhairava devotee's command, Siruthonda Nayanar, with Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammaiyar, goes outside the house and begins to call, "My son, come!" "My son, come!" The lady, who upholds her Lord's command with her head, Thiruvengattu Nangaiyar, also calls. "My precious jewel, Siraala! Come! The devotee of Shiva calls you to eat with him, so that we may be saved!" she cries out. Since the devotee commanded, "Call your son," the Nayanar called, "My son." Here, some mistakenly interpret this as Siruthonda Nayanar calling out of paternal affection, based on their own consciousness. That is not the case. The moment the Bhairava devotee spoke of eating the human sacrifice, Siruthonda Nayanar considered his son as the devotee's food and completely abandoned his attachment to him as his son. Therefore, "My son" here does not refer to that relationship. He calls him because the devotee commanded him to call his son. We must also understand that the mother called her son out of duty to her Lord's command, not with the expectation that the child would return. And how do they call him? They say, "I am not calling; the devotee is calling. Come for his sake." Not only do they call, but they cry out. "My jewel, Siraala! Come!" they shout loudly. This indicates their eagerness. It indicates the intensity of their desire to feed the devotees the sacred food. As they called out, by the grace of the Lord, hearing their call, he came running as if from school, the unique son Siraaladevar, who shone with unparalleled beauty. The mother picked him up, embraced him, held him close to her, and gave him to her husband. Why? Because they rejoiced with the joy of having received the blessing of feeding the devotee of the Lord who had burned the three cities. Therefore, they did not rejoice because the son had returned. Sekkizhar Peruman here teaches us about the great mental state of Siruthonda Nayanar, saying that they rejoiced because, through his coming, they had received the blessing of feeding the devotee. The son who came running, he came with an unblemished form, having been concealed by the Lord and then returned by Him with beauty. Therefore, that beauty was divine and beyond compare. The mother picked up and embraced the son who came running and gave him to her husband, because he was her son given by the Lord, and because he was her son who had come to eat with the Lord. The 83rd verse. He quickly took the son who had come and went to feed the Lord. But before that, the Lord, the Bhairava, had disappeared. His mind troubled, he could not see him and was bewildered. He fell down, his mind in turmoil. He was startled to find that the cooked meat curry was also not in the vessel. As previously mentioned, Siruthonda Nayanar quickly entered the house with the son who had come, intending to feed the Lord. But before that, the Lord, the Bhairava, had disappeared from there. Siruthonda Nayanar's mind was troubled and bewildered, unable to see the Bhairava devotee. He fell down, his mind in turmoil. He was startled to find that the cooked meat curry was also not in the vessel. Verses 84 and 85. Siruthonda Nayanar searched for the Bhairava, who had a red body, black hair, and a rich cloak, asking, "Where has he hidden, without partaking of the sacred food for our salvation?" Confused, he came out of the house. Then, the hidden Lord, with the daughter of the mountain (Uma) and the son of Saravana (Muruga), appeared on a great white bull in the vast sky. The Lord of the host of Bhootas, sages, gods, Vidyadharas, and others praised Him. He appeared before Siruthondar, who had prepared the sweet curry and sacred food, and with the cool white moon adorning His crown, He bestowed His vast gaze of grace. Siruthonda Nayanar searched for the Bhairava, who had a red body, black hair, and a rich cloak, asking, "Where has he hidden, without partaking of the sacred food for our salvation?" Confused, he came out of the house while searching. At that moment, the hidden Lord, the Bhairava, appeared with Uma, the daughter of the mountain, and Murugapperuman, the son raised in Saravana. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Murugapperuman as "Saravanathu Thanaiyar" (the son of Saravana). Thus, the Lord appeared as Somaskanda Murthy, with Uma and Murugapperuman.

It is a great distinction that the Lord bestowed grace upon this great Siruthondar only at this one place. Mostly in the Periyapuranam, when the Lord comes to bestow grace upon the Nayanmars, we see Him appearing on a bull, with Uma. Here, we see that the Lord appeared as Somaskanda Murthy, with Himself, Uma, and Murugapperuman in the middle, only at this one place. We see that the Lord, along with Uma, came with Velmaindan, the son of Saravana, to bestow grace upon the devotee, upon Nangai (Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammaiyar), upon Santhanathathaiyar, and upon Siraaladevar. Saravana means a reed forest. A pond amidst a reed forest is called Saravana. Saram means reed, Vanam means forest. Therefore, "Saravanathu Thanaiyar" means the Kumarakkadavul (Lord Muruga) who was born in the Saravana pond. Thus, the Lord appeared with Uma and Saravanathu Thanaiyar. The leaders of the Shiva Bhoota Ganas, sages, gods, Vidyadharas, and all others praised Him. To bestow grace upon Siruthondar, Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammaiyar, Siraaladevanar, and Santhanathathaiyar, who had prepared the sweet curry and sacred food, the Lord appeared before them in the vast sky on a unique white bull. With the cool white moon adorning His crown, He bestowed His vast gaze of grace. Verses 86 and 87. The devotee, who had conquered with love, and his wife, son, and maid, saw the great life that appeared before them. Overwhelmed, their bones and minds melted, they fell, rose, and praised. Then the Lord, in His greatness, bestowed grace upon them, who were great in their worthiness. The Lord with the Kondrai flower in His hair, and the noble lady who shared His body, and the victorious spear-wwielding son, took the devotee, his wife, his long-lived son, and his maid, who stood at their lotus feet, never to be separated, and carried them away with Him. Siruthondar, the devotee who had conquered worldly attachments through the intensity of his love, and his wife, Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammaiyar, who was perfectly suited to him, and their son, Siraaladeva Nayanar, who was perfectly suited to them, all saw the great life, the divine vision of Lord Shiva, who appeared before them. Overwhelmed, they lost themselves in that vision. Their bones and minds melted, they fell, rose, and praised. Then the Lord, in His greatness, bestowed grace upon them, who were great in their worthiness. Siruthonda Nayanar was a conqueror of attachments, having overcome the paternal affection that even the wise find difficult to transcend. His wife, Ammaiyar, was perfectly suited to him, having performed the sacred service as instructed, "the mother holding while the father cut." Their son was perfectly suited to them. All of them attained a great life. They saw the divine vision. Earlier, this same Lord had appeared, but they had not fully seen His nature. But now, He appeared fully and bestowed His gaze of grace, and they saw Him completely. The Lord bestows grace upon all three of them. And He also bestows grace upon Santhanathathaiyar. Siraaladeva Nayanar is praised as "Needu Maganar" (the long-lived son). That is a special epithet for Siraaladevar. Santhanathathaiyar Ammaiyar is mentioned here as "Maganar Thathiyar" (the son's maid). There are five types of mothers. Since she raised Siraaladeva Nayanar as a nurturing mother, and also served the devotees, the Lord bestows liberation upon Santhanathathaiyar, just as He did upon Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammaiyar, Siraaladeva Nayanar's mother. Siruthonda Deva Nayanar, Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammaiyar, Siraaladeva Nayanar, and Santhanathathaiyar Ammaiyar, all of them, were taken by the Lord, the Goddess, and Murugapperuman to their Shiva Loka, to remain in eternal liberation, always worshipping them without separation. Thus, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman concludes this Puranam. Now, the concluding verse, the 88th verse. In it, Sekkizhar Peruman concludes the Puranam of Siruthonda Nayanar and sets the stage for the next Puranam. The 88th verse is as follows: "Bearing on my head the feet of Siruthonda Nayanar, who received the great blessing of offering his unique son, who was without blemish, as meat to the Lord who wears the Ganga on His head, knowing him to be a devotee, we shall now praise the greatness of the Nayanar who will be known as Kalarittrarivar." Thus, the 88th concluding verse of this Puranam is set. Bearing on my head the feet of Siruthonda Nayanar, who received the great blessing of offering his unique son, who was without blemish, as meat to the Lord who wears the Ganga on His head, knowing him to be a devotee, Sekkizhar Peruman concludes the Puranam of Siruthonda Nayanar and sets the stage for the next Puranam, that of Kalarittrarivar Nayanar, saying, "We shall now praise the greatness of the Nayanar who will be known as Kalarittrarivar by those feet." We too, bowing at the feet of Siruthonda Nayanar, conclude his Puranam and will now continue to reflect on the Puranam of Kalarittrarivar Nayanar. Praise, praise to the sacred feet of our Lord Siruthonda Deva Nayanar! Praise, praise to the sacred feet of our Goddess Thiruvengattu Nangai Ammaiyar! Praise, praise to the sacred feet of our Lord Siraaladeva Nayanar! Praise, praise to the sacred feet of our Goddess Santhanathathaiyar Ammaiyar! Praise, praise to the sacred feet of Sundaramurthy Swamigal! Praise, praise to the sacred feet of Nambiyandar Nambigal! Praise, praise to the sacred feet of the divine Sekkizhar Peruman! Praise, praise to the golden feet of the commentator! Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.
